All experiments are conducted using PyTorch. All the training and experiments are conducted on four NVIDIA RTX8000 GPU.

We use the score parameterization introduced in (
45
):

s_{t}^{\theta,\ell}(x)=\dfrac{2\alpha_{T_{f}-t}}{1+\alpha_{T_{f}-t}}-\dfrac{4\alpha_{T_{f}-t}d_{t}^{\theta,\ell}(x)}{1-\alpha_{T_{f}-t}^{2}}\;,

(53)

where the neural network
d_{t}^{\theta,\ell}(x)
aims to approximate
d_{t}^{\ell}(x)=\mathbb{P}(\overrightarrow{X}_{0}^{\ell}\neq x^{\ell}\big|\overrightarrow{X}_{T_{f}-t}=x)
. Since the output of the neural network is
d_{t}^{\theta}(x)\in(0,1)^{d}
, we add a sigmoid activation function at the last layer.

We consider various loss configurations
\mathfrak{L}_{\varpi},\mathfrak{L}_{\varpi}^{w}
as introduced in (
26
), (
52
), with
6
choices of coefficients
(\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2},\varpi_{3})
normalized in the 2-simplex
\Delta_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We test all
2^{3}-1=7
possible non-empty combinations, minus the single
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{e}}
loss combination (
\varpi_{2}=1
), as the latter only acts as entropic regularization and does not perform well by itself. This lets us study the synergies between the different loss terms.

D.1
Small dimension data

We first conduct experiments on a discrete data distribution
p
supported on
\{0,1\}^{d}
. Each component of
X=(X_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\sim p
is independently distributed as a Bernoulli distribution with parameter
p_{i}
:

p(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}(x_{i})\;,

(54)

where the map
i\mapsto p_{i}
forms a sawtooth-like pattern, oscillating linearly between
0.05
and
0.95
, as can be seen in
Figure

6
.

Figure 6:
Sawtooth pattern used to define
i\mapsto p_{i}
, plotted with
d=16
. Values oscillate linearly from
0.05
to
0.95
, and back.

For training, we use
20\,000
datapoints resampled at each epoch, and a batch size of
1024
. We train each model for
300
epochs, using AdamW with a learning rate of 1e-3.
We employ a network composed of multiple MLP blocks:
4
residual blocks, each consisting of two feed-forward layers of width
256
; layer normalization and SiLU activations in each block; a feed-forward embedding for the timesteps, mapping
\mathbb{R}
to a hidden dimension of
256
, whose output is then injected into each residual block by an additional MLP of dimension
256\times 256
.

For evaluation, we estimate each distribution with
20{,}000
samples, and draw
1000
vectors uniformly on the simplex
\Delta_{d}
to compute our
SWD
metric (see
Section

D.3
).

D.2
Image data

We work on the binarized MNIST dataset, which we scale from
28\times 28
to
32\times 32
in order to fit in the U-Net architecture. We set the pixel value to
0
if its intensity is below
0.5
, and to
1
otherwise.

We compare DMPM to MD4 (masked diffusion, as in
Shi et al. (2024)
) and DFM (discrete flow matching, as in
Gat et al. (2024)
). We reimplement MD4 with the cosine schedule and the algorithms given in Appendix F of
Shi et al. (2024)
. We implement DFM based on the Pytorch implementation in
https://github.com/gle-bellier/discrete-fm
, and we use corrector sampling for better results.

For DMPM, we are using the cosine time-schedule and time horizon
T_{f}=3
. For both MD4 and DFM, we set the mask value to the integer
2
.

To establish a fair comparison, we use the same network model for every method. We use a U-Net following the implementation of
(
Nichol & Dhariwal, 2021
)
available in
https://github.com/openai/improved-diffusion
. We dimension the network as follows.

The first layer is an embedding layer of output dimension
32
and input dimension
d_{\text{input}}
, where
d_{\text{input}}=2
for DMPM (input values are either
0
and
1
) and
d_{\text{input}}=3
for MD4 and DFM (input values are either
0,1
or the mask value
2
).

We set the hidden layers to
[128,256,256,256]
, fix the number of residual blocks to
2
at each level, and add self-attention block at resolution
16\times 16
, using 4 heads. We use an exponential moving average with a rate of
0.99
. We use the silu activation function at every layer. Timestep
t
is fed to the model through the Transformer sinusoidal position embedding.

For DMPM and MD4, we set the number of output channels to
1
and add a sigmoid activation at the last layer. For DFM, we set the output channels to
3
and apply softmax channel-wise.

The optimizer is AdamW with learning rate 5e-4. We use the StepLR scheduler which scales the learning rate by
\gamma=.99
every
400
steps. We train on MNIST for
120\,000
steps with batch size
256
. A single training run on MNIST takes approximately 6 hours per GPU, and requires about 6-12GB of VRAM for our settings.

To assess the quality of our generative models, we compute our metrics between
4\,000
real images and
4\,000
generated images. Generating
4\,000
images with
1\,000
reverse steps takes approximately
1
hour on one GPU.

D.3
Metrics

For low-dimensional data, we use a custom sliced Wasserstein metric. For image data, in addition to the classical FID metric, we use a
\text{F}_{1}^{\text{DC}}
summary score, based on the density and coverage metrics.

\text{F}_{1}^{\text{DC}.}
as summary metric of density-coverage
The density and coverage metrics are introduced in the setting of generative models by
(
Naeem et al., 2020
)
. They assess the overlap of sample distributions using local geometric structures. Density measures how much the generated distribution is contained in the original data distribution (measuring quality), and coverage measures how much of the original data distribution is covered by the generated distribution (diversity).

These metrics are improvements of the precision and recall metrics for generative models
(
Kynkäänniemi et al., 2019
)
. They offer different measures to characterize the performance of generative models. For instance they can decorrelate the negative effect of mode collapse from the negative effect of noisy/blurry generations, each of them decreasing respectively coverage and density, and have been of importance in recent studies, e.g., in heavy-tailed generative modeling
(
Shariatian et al., 2025
;
Yoon et al., 2023
)
.

We consider a single summary
\text{F}_{1}^{\text{DC}}
score, which we define as the harmonic mean of these two values:

\text{F}_{1}^{\text{DC}}=2\cdot\dfrac{\text{density}\cdot\text{coverage}}{\text{density}+\text{coverage}}\;.

(55)

Sliced Wasserstein metric
SWD
.

Since the state space of our dataset over
\{0,1\}^{d}
is of size
2^{d}
, we cannot work with histogram-based metrics, which would require exponentially many samples when
d
increases.

We address this issue with our sliced Wasserstein metric
SWD
. This metric is defined between distributions
\mu,\nu
on
\{0,1\}^{d}
as:

\text{SWD}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{\Delta_{d}}\text{W}\left(u_{\#}\mu,u_{\#}\nu\right)\text{d}u\;,

(56)

where, for
u\in\Delta_{d}
, the pushforward
u_{\#}
is derived from the function

x\in\{0,1\}^{d}\mapsto\langle u,x\rangle\in[0,1]\;.

(57)

Simple Monte-Carlo averages are used to evaluate the integral with respect to the uniform distribution over the simplex
\Delta_{d}
, and we compute the Wasserstein distance between the pushforward measures with the
pyemd
package
(
Laszuk, 2017
)
.

Appendix E
Additional results

In this section, we give grid images of generated samples for DMPM models trained on binarized MNIST, with the loss
\mathfrak{L}_{1/3,1/3,1/3}^{w}
.

Figure 7:
Default DMPM sampler, 25 reverse steps, cosine time-schedule, linear flip-schedule dimensioned for
1000
total bit flips.

Figure 8:
Denoise-renoise sampler, 200 reverse steps, cosine time-schedule.

Appendix F
Convergence of DMPMs

The proof of DMPMs’ convergence requires understanding the backward dynamic under the canonical process point of view equivalent to the transition matrix point of view we provided in
Section

1
. We provide first some essential preliminaries on Poisson measures and the corresponding Itô’s formula.

F.1
Some basic facts on stochastic calculus for CTMCs

F.1.1
Point processes and Poisson point processes

Let
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
be a measurable space. Let
\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{Y}}
be the set of non-negative (possibly infinite) integer-valued measures on
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Y}}
be the smallest
\sigma
-field on
\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{Y}}
with respect to which the mappings
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{Y}}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{B})\in\mathbb{N}\cup\left\{+\infty\right\}
,
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{Y}
, are measurable.

Definition F.1

(Poisson random measure)
.

An
(\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{Y}},\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Y}})
-valued random variable
\boldsymbol{\mu}
(
i.e.
, a mapping
\boldsymbol{\mu}:\Omega\to\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{Y}}
defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
which is
\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Y}}
-measurable) is called a
Poisson random measure
if

•

for each
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{B})
is Poisson distributed;
i.e.
,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{B})=n)=\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\mathsf{B})^{n}\exp[-\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\mathsf{B})]/n!
for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
where
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\mathsf{B})=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{B})]
,
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Note that if
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(B)=\infty
, we understand that
\boldsymbol{\mu}(B)=\infty
a.s.

•

If
\mathsf{B}_{1},\mathsf{B}_{2}.\ldots,\mathsf{B}_{n}\in\mathcal{Y}
are disjoint, then
\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{B}_{1}),\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{B}_{2}),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{B}_{n})
are mutually independent.

Remark that it is easy to show that
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is a non-negative measure on
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
that uniquely determines the distribution of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
by the monotone class theorem; see
(
Ikeda & Watanabe, 2014
, Chapter I, Section 9)
. It is called the
mean measure
or the
intensity measure
of the Poisson random measure
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
be a measurable space. We define a
point function

\mathfrak{p}
on
\mathsf{X}
by a mapping
\mathfrak{p}:D_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\mathsf{X}
, where its domain
D_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a countable subset of
(0,\infty)
.

\mathfrak{p}
defines a non-negative (possibly infinite) integer-valued measure

N_{\mathfrak{p}}
on
(0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X}
endowed with the product
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{B}((0,\infty))\times\mathcal{X}
by

N_{\mathfrak{p}}((0,t]\times\mathsf{U})=\mathrm{Card}\left\{s\in D_{\mathfrak{p}}\,:\,\;s\leqslant t,\;\mathfrak{p}(s)\in\mathsf{U}\right\},\quad t>0\;,\quad\mathsf{U}\in\mathcal{X}\;.

A point process is obtained by randomizing the notion of point functions. Let
\Pi_{\mathsf{X}}
be the set of point functions with values in
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathcal{B}(\Pi_{\mathsf{X}})
be the smallest
\sigma
-field on
\Pi_{\mathsf{X}}
with respect to which the mappings
\mathfrak{p}\mapsto N_{\mathfrak{p}}((0,t]\times\mathsf{U}),t>0,\mathsf{U}\in\mathcal{X}
, are measurable.

Definition F.2

(Point process)
.

A
point process

\mathbf{p}
on
\mathsf{X}
is a
(\Pi_{\mathsf{X}},\mathcal{B}(\Pi_{\mathsf{X}}))
-valued random variable, that is, a mapping
\mathbf{p}:\Omega\to\Pi_{\mathsf{X}}
defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
which is
\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{B}(\Pi_{\mathsf{X}})
-measurable.

A point process
\mathbf{p}
is called
stationary
if for every
t>0
,
\mathbf{p}
and
\theta_{t}\mathbf{p}
have the same probability law, where
\theta_{t}\mathbf{p}
is defined by
D_{\theta_{t}\mathbf{p}}=\left\{s\in(0,\infty)\,:\,s+t\in D_{\mathbf{p}}\right\}
and
(\theta_{t}\mathbf{p})(s)=\mathbf{p}(s+t)
.

Definition F.3

(Poisson point process)
.

A point process
\mathbf{p}
is called
Poisson
if
N_{\mathbf{p}}
is a Poisson random measure on
(0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X}
. A Poisson point process is stationary if and only if its intensity measure
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)=\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)]
is of the form

\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)=\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{n}(\mathrm{d}x)

for some measure
\mathrm{n}
on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
.

F.1.2
Stochastic Integral with respect to Point Process

Here, we review the construction and definition of stochastic integral with respect to Point Processes for completeness; see
(
Ikeda & Watanabe, 2014
, Chapter II, Section 3)
.

Let
(\Omega,(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space, where
\mathbb{P}
is defined on the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all
\mathcal{F}_{t},t\geqslant 0
and
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
satisfies the usual conditions (
i.e.
, right-continuity and completeness).

A point process
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{p}(t))
on
\mathsf{X}
defined on
\Omega
is called
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-adapted
if for every
t>0
and
\mathsf{U}\in\mathcal{X}
,
N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})=\sum_{s\in D_{\mathbf{p}},s\leqslant t}\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{U}}(\mathbf{p}(s))
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable.
\mathbf{p}
is called
\sigma
-finite
if there exist
\mathsf{U}_{n}\in\mathcal{X},n=1,2,\ldots
such that
\cup_{n}\mathsf{U}_{n}=\mathsf{X}
and
\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U}_{n})]<\infty
for all
t>0
and
n=1,2,\ldots
. For a given
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-adapted,
\sigma
-finite point process
\mathbf{p}
, let
\Gamma_{\mathbf{p}}=\left\{U\in\mathcal{X};\;\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})]<\infty\text{ for all }t>0\right\}
. If
\mathsf{U}\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{p}}
, then
t\in[0,\infty)\mapsto N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})
is an adapted, integrable increasing process and hence there exists a natural integrable increasing process
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})
such that
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}:t\in(0,\infty)\mapsto\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})=N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})-\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})
is a martingale.

Definition F.4
.

An
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-adapted point process
\mathbf{p}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is said to be
of the class
\mathrm{(QL)}

(quasi left-continuous) (w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
) if it is
\sigma
-finite and there exists

\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}
such that

1.

for
\mathsf{U}\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{p}}
,
t\in(0,\infty)\mapsto\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})
is a continuous
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-adapted increasing process,

2.

for each
t\in(0,\infty)
and a.a.
\omega\in\Omega
,
\mathsf{U}\mapsto\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})
is a
\sigma
-finite measure on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
,

3.

for
\mathsf{U}\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{p}}
,
t\in(0,\infty)\mapsto\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})=N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})-\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-martingale.

The random measure
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}
is called the
compensator
of the point process
\mathbf{p}
(or
N_{\mathbf{p}}
).

Definition F.5
.

A point process
\mathbf{p}
is called an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-Poisson point process
if it is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-adapted,
\sigma
-finite Poisson point process such that
\left\{N_{\mathbf{p}}(t+h,\mathsf{U})-N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})\right\}_{h>0,\mathsf{U}\in\mathcal{X}}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
.

An
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-Poisson point process is of class
\mathrm{(QL)}
if and only if
t\mapsto\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})]
is continuous for
\mathsf{U}\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{p}}
; in this case, the compensator
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}
is given by
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})=\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathbf{p}}(t,\mathsf{U})]
.

Theorem F.6
.

Let
\mathbf{p}
be a point process of class
\mathrm{(QL)}
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
on some state space
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
such that its compensator
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)
is a
non-random

\sigma
-finite measure on
[0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X}
. Then
\mathbf{p}
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-Poisson point process. If, in particular,
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)=\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{n}(\mathrm{d}x)
where
\mathrm{n}(\mathrm{d}x)
is a non-random
\sigma
-finite measure on
\mathsf{X}
,
\mathbf{p}
is a stationary
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-Poisson point process with
\mathrm{n}
as its characteristic measure.

We are now going to discuss stochastic integrals w.r.t. a given point process of the class
\mathrm{(QL)}
. For this it is convenient to generalize the notion of predictable processes.

Definition F.7

(Predictable functions)
.

A real function
f(t,x,\omega)
defined on
[0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X}\times\Omega
is called
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-predictable if the mapping
(t,x,\omega)\mapsto f(t,x,\omega)
is
\mathcal{S}/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
-measurable where
\mathcal{S}
is the smallest
\sigma
-field on
[0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X}\times\Omega
w.r.t. which all
g
having the following properties are measurable:

1.

for each
t>0,(x,\omega)\mapsto g(t,x,\omega)
is
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable;

2.

for each
(x,\omega)
,
t\in[0,\infty)\mapsto g(t,x,\omega)
is left continuous.

We introduce the following classes

\displaystyle F_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\{f(t,x,\omega);f\text{ is }(\mathcal{F}_{t})\text{-predictable and for each }t>0,\;\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}|f(s,x,\omega)|N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)<\infty\text{ a.s.}\right\}\;,

(58)

\displaystyle F^{1}_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\{f(t,x,\omega);f\text{ is }(\mathcal{F}_{t})\text{-predictable and for each }t>0,\;\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}|f(s,x,\cdot)|\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right]<\infty\right\}\;.

Theorem F.8

(Stochastic integral)
.

For
f\in F_{\mathbf{p}}
, the stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)
is well-defined a.s. and equals the absolutely convergent sum,

\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{s\leqslant t,s\in D_{\mathbf{p}}}f(s,\mathbf{p}(s),\cdot)\;.

We then denote the stochastic integral w.r.t.
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}
as

\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;,

and the process
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}|f(s,x,\cdot)|\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-local martingale.

Now let
f\in F^{1}_{\mathbf{p}}
, the stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}|f(s,x,\cdot)|N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}|f(s,x,\cdot)|\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right]\;.

This implies, in particular, that
F^{1}_{\mathbf{p}}\subset F_{\mathbf{p}}
. Then the stochastic integral
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)
with
f\in F^{1}_{\mathbf{p}}
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-true martingale.

F.1.3
Itô’s formula for point process

Itô’s formula is one of the most important tools in the study of semi-martingales. It provides us with the differential-integral calculus for sample functions of stochastic processes.

Let
(\Omega,(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space given as above and let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
be a measurable space. Define a
d
-dimensional semi-martingale
\mathbf{X}_{t}=(\mathbf{X}^{1}_{t},\mathbf{X}^{2}_{t},\ldots,\mathbf{X}^{d}_{t})
adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
, taking values in
\mathsf{X}
by

\mathbf{X}^{i}_{t}=\mathbf{X}^{i}_{0}+M^{i}_{t}+A^{i}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f^{i}(s,x,\cdot)N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)+\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}g^{i}(s,x,\cdot)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;,

for
i=1,2,\ldots,d
, where

•

each
(M^{i}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}\in\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\text{c,loc}}
with

\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\text{c,loc}}=\left\{(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0};(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}\text{ is a locally square integrable }(\mathcal{F}_{t})\text{-martingale},M_{0}=0\text{ a.s. };t\mapsto X_{t}\text{ is continuous a.s.}\right\}\;;

•

each
(A^{i}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is a continuous
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-adapted process satisfying
A^{i}_{0}=0
and for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, the sample path
t\mapsto A_{t}^{i}(\omega)
has bounded variation on each finite interval;

•

\mathbf{p}
is a point process of the class
\mathrm{(QL)}
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
on some state such that
f^{i}(t,x,\omega)g^{j}(t,x,\omega)=0
for any
(i,j,t,x,\omega)\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{2}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X}\times\Omega
; furthermore, we assume that
a constant
C>0
exists such that

|g^{i}(t,x,\omega)|\leqslant C\quad\text{for all }(i,t,x,\omega)\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X}\times\Omega\;;

•

each
\mathbf{X}^{i}_{0}
is an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable.

Denote also
f=(f^{1},\ldots,f^{d})
and
g=(g^{1},\ldots,g^{d})
.

Theorem F.9

(Itô’s formula,
(
Ikeda & Watanabe, 2014
, Chapter II, Section 5)
)
.

Let
F
be a function of class
\mathrm{C}^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
be a
d
-dimensional semi-martingale given above. Then the stochastic process
(F(\mathbf{X}_{t}))_{t\geqslant 0}
is also a semi-martingale w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
and the following formula holds:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{X}_{t})-F(\mathbf{X}_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{s})\mathrm{d}M^{i}_{s}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{s})\mathrm{d}A^{i}_{s}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial F^{2}}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}(\mathbf{X}_{s})\mathrm{d}\langle M^{i},M^{j}\rangle_{s}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(\mathbf{X}_{s-}+f(s,x,\cdot))-F(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\right\}N_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(\mathbf{X}_{s-}+g(s,x,\cdot))-F(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\right\}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(\mathbf{X}_{s}+g(s,x,\cdot))-F(\mathbf{X}_{s})-\sum_{i=1}^{d}g^{i}(s,x,\cdot)\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{X}_{s})\right\}\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;.

Corollary F.10

(Itô’s isometry)
.

Let
N_{\mathbf{p}}
,
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}
and
\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}
given as above. Then the following holds for real-valued functions
f\in F_{\mathbf{p}}^{1}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\omega)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f^{2}(s,x,\omega)\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right]\;.

Proof of
Corollary

F.10
.

Denote the process
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
by

\mathbf{X}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\omega)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;,

and apply Itô’s formula (see
Theorem

F.9
) for function
F(x)=x^{2}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2}-\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{(\mathbf{X}_{s-}+f(s,x,\omega))^{2}-\mathbf{X}_{s-}^{2}\right\}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{(\mathbf{X}_{s}+f(s,x,\omega))^{2}-\mathbf{X}_{s}^{2}-2\mathbf{X}_{s}f(s,x,\omega)\right\}\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;,\quad\text{for $t\geqslant 0$}\;.

Taking expectations on both sides and using the martingale property of the stochastic integral with respect to
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2}-\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f^{2}(s,x,\omega)\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right]\;.

Plugging the formula of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
into the above and noting that
\mathbf{X}_{0}=0
yields the desired equation,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\omega)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f^{2}(s,x,\omega)\hat{N}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right]\;,

and concludes the proof.
∎

F.1.4
Application to CTMCs

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
be a measurable space with
\mathsf{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
let
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
be a CTMC on
\mathsf{X}
associated with the jump rate function

r:(0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and the kernel
k_{t}(X_{t-},x)
determining the probability of jumping into
x\in\mathsf{X}
at time
t
, given a jump occurs from a current state
X_{t-}
. The generator
\mathfrak{q}_{t}
is defined by
\mathfrak{q}_{t}(X_{t-},x)=r_{t}(X_{t-})k_{t}(X_{t-},x)
represents the rate of jumping from the current state
X_{t-}
to the new state
x\in\mathsf{X}
at time
t\in(0,T_{f}]
.
The CTMC
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
defines a point process
\mathbf{p}_{X}=(\mathbf{p}_{X}(t))_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{X}:D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}\subset(0,T_{f}]\to\mathsf{X}\;,\quad\mathbf{p}_{X}(t)=X_{t}\;,\,t\in D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}\;,

with
D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}
is the set of jump times of
(X_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
. We observe that
\mathbf{p}_{X}
describes the new state after jumping at time
t\in(0,T_{f}]
and it constructs a corresponding random measure
N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)
on
(0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}
by

\displaystyle N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}((0,t]\times\mathsf{U})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Card}\left\{s\in D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}:s\leqslant t,\mathbf{p}_{X}(s)\in\mathsf{U}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}}\updelta_{(s,X_{s})}((0,t]\times\mathsf{U})\hskip 42.67912pt\text{for }t\in(0,T_{f}],\quad\mathsf{U}\in\mathcal{X}\;,

that counts the total jumps into
\mathsf{U}\in\mathcal{X}
occurring during
(0,t]
. Then the random compensator
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
of
N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}
is given by

\displaystyle\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{X}}\mathfrak{q}(X_{t-},y)\mathds{1}_{X_{t-}\neq y}\delta_{y}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}t\;,

since the corresponding compensated measure

\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)=N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)-\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)

is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-martingale,
where
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
denotes the right-continuous and complete natural filtration generated by the process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
.

Indeed, we can show the martingale property of
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
as follows.
For any function
f\in F^{1}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}
, where the class
F^{1}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}
is given in (
58
)
, define the following stochastic integrals:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<s\leqslant t\\
s\in D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}\end{subarray}}f(s,\mathbf{p}_{X}(s),\cdot)\;,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(s,x,\cdot)\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;.

Then for
0\leqslant s<t\leqslant T_{f}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{s}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(z,x,\cdot)N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s<z\leqslant t\\
z\in D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}\end{subarray}}f(z,X_{z},\cdot)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{s}^{t}f(z,x,\cdot)r(X_{z-})\frac{\mathfrak{q}(X_{z-},x)}{r(X_{z-})}\mathds{1}_{X_{z-}\neq x}\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}x\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(z,x,\cdot)\mathfrak{q}(X_{z-},x)\mathds{1}_{X_{z-}\neq x}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(z,x,\cdot)\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]\;,

meaning that
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
is indeed the compensator of the random measure
N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}
.
With those notations in hand, we can decompose the CTMC
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
as

X_{t}=X_{0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<s\leqslant t\\
s\in D_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}\end{subarray}}(X_{s}-X_{s-})=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}(x-X_{s-})N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;,\quad\text{for }t\in[0,T_{f}]\;,

under the assumption
f(s,x,\omega):=x-\omega_{s-}\in F_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}
, where
F_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}
defined in (
58
). Applying Itô’s formula to this process, for any function
F
in
\mathrm{C}^{2}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
, we get that

\displaystyle F(X_{t})-F(X_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(X_{s-}+x-X_{s-})-F(X_{s-})\right\}N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(x)-F(X_{s-})\right\}N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

Expressing the random measure as
N_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}=\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}+\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
and plugging it into the formula above yield

\displaystyle F(X_{t})-F(X_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(x)-F(X_{s-})\right\}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(x)-F(X_{s-})\right\}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;.

In other words, the process

\displaystyle\left(F(X_{t})-F(X_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left\{F(x)-F(X_{s-})\right\}\mathds{1}_{X_{s-}\neq x}\mathfrak{q}(X_{s-},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}s\right)_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}

is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-local martingale as the compensated measure
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{p}_{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
was shown to be an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-martingale in the previous computation.
It follows that for the CTMC
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
with generator
\mathfrak{q}
, Itô’s formula asserts that the process

\left(F(X_{t})-F(X_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{q}F(X_{s-})\mathrm{d}s\right)_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}

is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-local martingale for any function
F\in\mathrm{C}^{2}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
. This result aligns with Dynkin’s formula.

F.2
Canonical process point of view

We now want to give a description of the time reversal process as a controlled process like in the continuous setting in
Conforti et al. (2025)
.

To this purpose, we consider the following canonical setting.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}=\mathbb{D}(\left[0,T_{f}\right];\mathsf{X})
be the canonical space of all càdlàg (right-continuous with left limits) paths from
\left[0,T_{f}\right]
to
\mathsf{X}=\{0,1\}^{d}
, and let
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
be the canonical process defined by

\mathbf{X}_{t}(\omega)=\omega_{t},\quad\text{ for }t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]\,,\,(\omega_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}\in\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\;.

We endow this space with the canonical filtration, that is, the right-continuous and complete augmentation of the filtration generated by
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
, denoted by
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
.

For any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
we denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}
the corresponding expectation. For any
t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}_{t}
, the distribution of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
under
\mathbb{P}
.

As usual convention, we simply denote the the random variable
\omega\mapsto U(\omega,t,x)
as
U(t,x)

for a predictable process
U:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}
.

For any random generator
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and predictable process
U:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, the new random generator
U\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as
(U\mathbf{q})(\omega,t,x,y):=U(\omega,t,y)\mathbf{q}(\omega,t,x,y)
for
x\neq y
.

For convenience, we denote by
\mathbb{F}(\mathsf{X})
the set of functions from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

We now follow the approach for time reversal used in
Léonard (2012)
to characterize the distribution of
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
as the solution of a martingale problem.

Definition F.11

(Martingale problem)
.

Let
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}
be a non-homogeneous predictable random generator. We say that
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
solves
the Martingale problem

\mathrm{MP}(\mathbf{q})
with initial condition
\mu_{0}
, and write
\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathbf{q})
, if under
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbf{X}_{0}
has distribution
\mu_{0}
and
the process

\displaystyle\left(f(\mathbf{X}_{t})-f(\mathbf{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{q}(s)f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\mathrm{d}s\right)_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}

is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
-local martingale for any function
f\in\mathbb{F}(\mathsf{X})
, where we denote
\mathbf{q}(t)f(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{X}}\mathbf{q}(\omega,t,x,y)f(y)
.

Note that by Itô’s formula and
Section

F.1.4
, if under
\mathbb{P}
,
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
is a CTMC with generator
\mathbf{q}
, then
\mathbb{P}
solves
\mathrm{MP}(\mathbf{q})
.

Recall that for the forward process under consideration, the generator
\overrightarrow{q}
is defined as

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{q}(x,y):

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}~~~\lambda\;,\quad&\text{if }y=\varphi^{(\ell)}(x)\text{ for some }\ell\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}\;,\\
-\lambda d\;,\quad&\text{if }y=x\;,\\
~~~0\;,\quad&\text{otherwise}\;,\end{cases}

(59)

where
\varphi^{(\ell)}:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}
is the function which flips the
\ell
-th component for
\ell\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
.
Note that by (
15
),
\gamma^{d}=\operatorname{Unif}(\mathsf{X})
is an invariant distribution for the CTMC with generator
\overrightarrow{q}
,
i.e.
, for any measurable function
f
,
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\overrightarrow{p}_{t}f(x)\gamma^{d}(x)=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{X}}f(x)\gamma^{d}(x)
.
In fact the transition density
\overrightarrow{p}_{t}
is reversible with respect to
\gamma^{d}
for any
t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]
using (
15
),
i.e.
, for any
x,y\in\mathsf{X}
and
t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]
,

\gamma^{d}(x)\overrightarrow{p}_{t}(x,y)=\gamma^{d}(y)\overrightarrow{p}_{t}(y,x)\;.

As a result, we get that for any
0\leqslant t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}\leqslant T_{f}
, under
\overrightarrow{R}
, where

\overrightarrow{R}
denoted the distribution of the CTMC with generator
\overrightarrow{q}
started at stationarity
\gamma^{d}
,
(\mathbf{X}_{t_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{t_{n}})
has the same distribution as
(\mathbf{X}_{T_{f}-t_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{T_{f}-t_{n}})
and therefore the reference path measure
\overrightarrow{R}
is reversible,
i.e.
,
\overrightarrow{R}=\overleftarrow{R}
, where
\overleftarrow{R}
is the distribution of
(\mathbf{X}_{T_{f}-t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
under
\overrightarrow{R}
.

Following
Section

F.1.4
, for any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
such that
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
is a CTMC with generator
\mathfrak{q}:[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
under
\mathbb{P}
, we denote by
\mathbf{p}_{\mathbf{X}}
the point process and by
N_{\mathbf{X}}
the random measure associated with
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
.

We also define for any
(\omega_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}\in\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}
:

\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}((\omega_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]},\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)=\mathrm{n}^{\mathfrak{q}}(t,\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}t\;,\quad\mathrm{n}^{\mathfrak{q}}(t,\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{X}}\mathds{1}_{\omega_{t-}\neq y}\mathfrak{q}_{t}(\omega_{t-},y)\updelta_{y}(\mathrm{d}x)\;.

(60)

By convention, we denote
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]},\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)
by
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)
which corresponds to the compensator of
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
under
\mathbb{P}
, if under this distribution
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
is a CTMC with generator
\mathfrak{q}:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Consequently, the compensated sum of jumps
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}=N_{\mathbf{X}}-\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
forms a martingale under
\mathbb{P}
.

F.2.1
Girsanov’s theorem

As proven in
Léonard (2012, Theorem 2.6-2.9)
, the relative entropy of two path measures associated to two jump processes can be expressed using the Young function

\varrho(a):=\mathrm{e}^{a}-a-1\;,\qquad\text{for $a\in\mathbb{R}$}\;.

Note that the convex conjugate
\varrho
is equal to

\varrho^{*}(b)=(b+1)\log(b+1)-b\;,\text{ for }b>-1\;,

with the convention
\varrho^{*}(-1)=1
and
\varrho^{*}(b)=\infty
, for
b<-1
. We recall that the functions
\varrho
and
\varrho^{*}
are respectively equivalent to
a^{2}/2
and
b^{2}/2
near zero.
Finally, we recall the definition of the function

h(a)=\varrho^{*}(a-1)\;,\qquad a\in\mathbb{R}\;.

Theorem F.12

(Girsanov’s theorem)
.

(
Léonard, 2012
, Theorem 2.6-2.9)

Let
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
verifying
\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})<\infty
. Then, there exists a unique predictable non-negative process
U:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}\to[0,\infty)

satisfying the integrability condition

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho^{*}(|U(t,x)-1|)\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)\right]<\infty\;,

(61)

and
\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{MP}(U\overrightarrow{q})
where
(U\overrightarrow{q})(\omega,t,x,y)=U(\omega,t,y)\overrightarrow{q}(x,y)
for
x\neq y
. Moreover, we have that

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}}((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]})=\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0})\exp\left(\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\log U(t,x)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\log U(t,x))\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)\right)\;,

and the
\mathrm{KL}
divergence reads as

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})=\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0}|\overrightarrow{R}_{0})+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}h(U(t,x))\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)\right]\;.

The proof of
Theorem

F.12
is given for completeness and is based on several technical lemmas, which we introduce in the following framework. Let
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
such that
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
is a CTMC with generator
\mathfrak{q}:[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i.e.
,
\sum_{y\in\mathsf{X}}\mathfrak{q}(t,x,y)=0
for any
(t,x)\in[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}
, and denote
\mathbf{p}_{\mathbf{X}}
,
N_{\mathbf{X}}
,
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
,
\mathrm{n}^{\mathfrak{q}}
and

\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
as in previous Section.

Let
\chi
be a
\mathbb{R}
-valued predictable process on
\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}
such that
\int_{[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi_{t}(\omega,x))\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
,
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-a.s. Define

\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi}:=\exp\left(\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi_{s}(\omega,x)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi_{s}(\omega,x))\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\right)\;,\quad\text{for }t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]\;.

(62)

Lemma F.13
.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
such that
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
is a CTMC with generator
\mathfrak{q}:[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. Assume that
\chi
is a
\mathbb{R}
-valued predictable process on
\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}
satisfying the integrability condition

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi_{s}(\omega,x))\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)<\infty\;.

(63)

Then
\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi_{s}(\omega,x)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)
is a local
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale. Moreover, the process
(\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
defined in (
62
) is a local
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale and a positive
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-supermartingale, which satisfies

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi}=\mathrm{Z}_{t-}^{\chi}\int_{\mathsf{X}}(\mathrm{e}^{\chi_{t}(\omega,x)}-1)\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)\;.

Proof of Lemma
F.13
.

By
Theorem

F.8
,
the process

M_{t}^{\chi}:=\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi_{s}(\omega,x)\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

is a local
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale. Denote
\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi}:=M^{\chi}_{t}-\int_{\left[0,t\right]}\beta_{s}\mathrm{d}s
with
\beta_{s}:=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi_{s}(\omega,x))\mathrm{n}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}(s,\mathrm{d}x)
.
Applying Itô’s formula provided in
Theorem

F.9
for the jump process
(\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
and for a function
f
of class
\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}f(\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi})

\displaystyle=\left[\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left[f(\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}+\chi_{t}(\omega,x))-f(\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi})-f^{\prime}(\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi})\cdot\chi_{t}(\omega,x)\right]\mathrm{n}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}(t,\mathrm{d}x)\right]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+f^{\prime}(\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi})\cdot\beta_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}M_{t}^{f}\;,\quad\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}\text{-a.s.}\;,

where
M_{t}
is given by

M_{t}^{f}=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\left[f(Y^{\chi}_{s-}+\chi_{s}(\omega,x))-f(Y^{\chi}_{s-})\right]\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

is a local
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale, since the integrand is
\mathbb{R}
-valued predictable process and
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
forms a martingale under
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Using this formula for
f(y)=\mathrm{e}^{y}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi}}

\displaystyle=\left[\int_{\mathsf{X}}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}+\chi_{t}(\omega,x)}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}}\cdot\chi_{t}(\omega,x))\mathrm{n}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}(t,\mathrm{d}x)\right]\mathrm{d}t-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}}\beta_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}M_{t}^{\exp}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}}\beta_{t}\mathrm{d}t-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}}\beta_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}M_{t}^{\exp}=\mathrm{d}M_{t}^{\exp}\;,\quad\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}\text{-a.s.}\;.

This implies
\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi}}
is a local
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale and, since
\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi}
is positive, we can conclude that
\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-supermartingale thanks to Fatou’s lemma.
In addition, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}M_{t}^{\exp}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{Y^{\chi}_{t-}+\chi_{t}(\omega,x)}-\mathrm{e}^{Y^{\chi}_{t-}}\right)\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t-}^{\chi}}\int_{\mathsf{X}}(\mathrm{e}^{\chi_{t}(\omega,x)}-1)\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)\;,

i.e.
,
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi}=\mathrm{Z}_{t-}^{\chi}\int_{\mathsf{X}}(\mathrm{e}^{\chi_{t}(\omega,x)}-1)\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)
and we conclude the proof of
Lemma

F.13
.
∎

We now define the stopping time for
k,j\geqslant 1
,

\sigma^{k}_{j}:=\inf\left\{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]\,:\,\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi_{s}(\omega,x))\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\geqslant k\text{ or }\chi_{t}(\omega,\omega_{t})\notin[-j,k]\right\}\;.

(64)

For
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
, let us denote
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}:=\mathbf{X}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}_{\#}\mathbb{P}
the law under
\mathbb{P}
of the process
\mathbf{X}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}
which is stopped at the stopping time
\sigma^{k}_{j}
.

Lemma F.14
.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
such that
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
is a CTMC with generator
\mathfrak{q}:[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and let
\chi
be a
\mathbb{R}
-valued predictable process on
\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}
satisfying the integrability condition (
63
).
Let
(\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
be defined in (
62
) and
\sigma^{k}_{j}
be defined in (
64
). For all
j,k\geqslant 1
, the process
(Z^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
defined as
\mathrm{Z}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}_{t}:=\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi^{k}_{j}}
, is a genuine
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale with
\chi^{k}_{j}=\mathds{1}_{[0,\sigma^{k}_{j}]}\chi
, and the measure
\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}
defined for any measurable function
F:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}[F((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]})]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}[F((\mathbf{X}_{t\wedge\sigma_{j}^{k}})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]})\mathrm{Z}_{T_{f}}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}]\;,\quad\textit{i.e.}\;,\quad\mathbb{Q}_{j}^{k}=\mathrm{Z}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}_{T_{f}}(\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}})^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}

is a probability measure on
\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}
which satisfies

\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{\left[0,\sigma^{k}_{j}\right]}\mathrm{e}^{\chi}{\mathfrak{q}})\;.

Proof of Lemma
F.14
.

Fix
j,k\geqslant 1
. We have

\mathrm{Z}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}_{t}=\exp\left(\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi^{k}_{j}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}-\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi^{k}_{j})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\;,

where
\chi^{k}_{j}=\mathds{1}_{\left[0,\sigma^{k}_{j}\right]}\chi
is predictable since
\chi
is predictable and
\mathds{1}_{\left[0,\sigma^{k}_{j}\right]}
is left continuous.
For simplicity, we drop the subscripts and superscripts and write
\tilde{\chi}=\chi^{k}_{j}
and
\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t}=\mathrm{Z}^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}_{t}
for the rest of the proof. From the definition of
\sigma^{k}_{j}
, we obtain that
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}

a.s.
,

\displaystyle\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\leqslant k\;,\quad\text{ and }\tilde{\chi}_{t}(\omega,\mathbf{X}_{t})\in[-j,k]\;,\text{ for any }t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]\;.

(65)

First, we prove that
(\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t})
is a
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale. From
Lemma

F.13
,
(\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t})
is a local martingale. Therefore, it is enough to show that for
t\in[0,T_{f}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}[\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t}^{p}]<\infty\;,\quad\text{for some }p>1\;.

For
p>1
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}^{p}_{t}=\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}-p\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\leqslant\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\;,

and

\displaystyle\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}-\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(p\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\geqslant\left.\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\middle/C(k,p,t)\right.\;,

for some finite deterministic constant
0<C(k,p,t)<\infty
. Indeed,
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-
a.s.
, for any
s\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]
, it holds that
\varrho(p\tilde{\chi}_{s})\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{k(p-1)}(\varrho(\tilde{\chi}_{s})+k+1)
since
\tilde{\chi}_{s}\leqslant k
and
p>1
. It yields that
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-
a.s.
, it holds

\displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(p\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\left(\mathrm{e}^{k(p-1)}\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}(\varrho(\tilde{\chi}_{s})+k+1)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{inequality}}{\leqslant}\exp\left(k\mathrm{e}^{k(p-1)}+(k+1)\mathrm{e}^{k(p-1)}\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}1\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\leqslant C(k,p,t)<\infty\;,

where the last inequality follows from the formula of
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
given in (
60
) and the fact that
\mathsf{X}
is finite.

This implies
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-a.s.,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}^{p}_{t}\leqslant\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}\right)\leqslant C(k,p,t)\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}-\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(p\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\;.

(66)

On the other hand, applying Lemma
F.13
for
p\tilde{\chi}
yields that
\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}-\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(p\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)
is a
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-supermartingale, and we get

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\exp\left(p\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}-\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(p\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\right]\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\exp\left(p\int_{[0,0]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\chi}_{s}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}-\int_{[0,0]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(p\tilde{\chi}_{s})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\right)\right]=1\;.

Plugging this estimate into (
66
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}[\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}^{p}_{t}]\leqslant C(k,p,t)<\infty\;,\quad\text{for any }t\in[0,T_{f}]\;,

which allow us to conclude that
(\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{T_{f}})
is a
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale
(
Zitkovic, 2015
, see,
e.g.
,)
. Thereby

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}[\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}[\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{0}]=1
for any
t\in[0,T_{f}]
and it follows
\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}
.

Now, we show the second claim of
Lemma

F.14
:

\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{\left[0,\sigma^{k}_{j}\right]}\mathrm{e}^{\chi}{\mathfrak{q}})\;.

Let
\tau
be a finitely valued stopping time which will be specified later, and
for any function
f\in\mathbb{F}(\mathsf{X})
,

we denote

\displaystyle\mathrm{F}_{t}=\sum_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}\{f(\mathbf{X}_{s})-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\}=\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\{f(x)-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\}N_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;,\quad\text{for }t\in[0,T_{f}]\;.

Recall that by
Lemma

F.13
, the martingale
(\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t})
satisfies the followings for
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-a.s.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t}=\mathds{1}_{\left[0,\sigma^{k}_{j}\right]}(t)\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t-}\int_{\mathsf{X}}(\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{t}(\omega,x)}-1)\tilde{N}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)\;.

We have

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\sum_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t\wedge\tau}\{f(\mathbf{X}_{s})-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\mathrm{F}_{t\wedge\tau}|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}\mathrm{F}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}-\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{0}\mathrm{F}_{0}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\;.

(67)

Let us denote the two-dimensional process
(\mathrm{I}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}=(\mathrm{I}^{1}_{t},\mathrm{I}^{2}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
, where

\mathrm{I}^{1}_{t}:=\mathrm{F}_{t}=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\underbrace{\left[f(x)-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\right]}_{=:v^{1}(s,x,\omega)}\tilde{N}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)+\underbrace{\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}{\left[f(x)-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\right]}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)}_{=:A^{1}_{t}}\;,

and

\mathrm{I}^{2}_{t}=\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t}=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}\underbrace{\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s-}(\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}-1)}_{:=v^{2}(s,x,\omega)}\tilde{N}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\;.

Let
v=(v^{1},v^{2})
and apply Itô’s formula (see
Theorem

F.9
) to the process
(\mathrm{I}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
using the function given by the product of the coordinates, treating
(A^{1}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
as a continuous, finite variation process adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
,

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\mathrm{F}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}-\mathrm{F}_{0}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{0}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\mathrm{I}^{1}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}\mathrm{I}^{2}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}-\mathrm{I}^{1}_{0}\mathrm{I}^{2}_{0}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\left\{(\mathrm{I}^{1}_{s-}+v^{1}(s,x,\omega))(\mathrm{I}^{2}_{s-}+v^{2}(s,x,\omega))-\mathrm{I}^{1}_{s-}\mathrm{I}^{2}_{s-}\right\}\tilde{N}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\left\{(\mathrm{I}^{1}_{s}+v^{1}(s,x,\omega))(\mathrm{I}^{2}_{s}+v^{2}(s,x,\omega))-\mathrm{I}^{1}_{s}\mathrm{I}^{2}_{s}-\mathrm{I}^{2}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)-\mathrm{I}^{1}_{s}v^{2}(s,x,\omega)\right\}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\mathrm{I}^{2}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\left\{\mathrm{I}^{2}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)+v^{1}(s,x,\omega)v^{2}(s,x,\omega)\right\}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\quad\text{(as $\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}$ is a $\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}$-martingale)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\left\{\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)+v^{1}(s,x,\omega)\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s-}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}-1\right)\right\}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\;,

where we reduce the stochastic integral w.r.t.
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
as it is a local
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale, since the integrand is
\mathbb{R}
-valued predictable process and
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
forms a martingale under
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Since
\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}=\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s-}
for Lebesgue almost all
s\in[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]

(
Mozumder, 2009
, Proposition 2.1)
and
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
is atomless in time, the calculation follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\mathrm{F}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}-\mathrm{F}_{0}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{0}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}-1\right)\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\;.

(68)

Denote
\mathrm{G}_{t}:=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathsf{X}}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)
. Applying Itô’s formula for the process
(\mathrm{G}_{t},\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t})
analogously as argued before, we obtain that

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}\mathrm{G}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}-\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{0}\mathrm{G}_{0}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\left\{\mathrm{G}_{s-}(\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s-}+v^{2}(s,x,\omega))-\mathrm{G}_{s-}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s-}\right\}\tilde{N}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\left\{(\mathrm{G}_{s}(\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}+v^{2}(s,x,\omega))-\mathrm{G}_{s}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}-\mathrm{G}_{s}v^{2}(s,x,\omega)\right\}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}]\times\mathsf{X}}\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{s}v^{1}(s,x,\omega)\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\;,

(69)

as the stochastic integral w.r.t.
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
is a local
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
-martingale, since the integrand is
\mathbb{R}
-valued predictable process and
\tilde{N}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
forms a martingale under
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Combining (
67
), (
68
) and (
69
) implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\sum_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t\wedge\tau}\{f(\mathbf{X}_{s})-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{q}}}\left[\tilde{\mathrm{Z}}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}\mathrm{G}_{t\wedge\tau\wedge\sigma^{k}_{j}}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\quad\text{(since $\mathrm{G}_{0}=0$)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\mathrm{G}_{t\wedge\tau}\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau]\times\mathsf{X}}\{f(x)-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau]\times\mathsf{X}}\{f(x)-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\}\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{X}_{s-}\neq x}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{s}(\omega,x)}{\mathfrak{q}}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{s-},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}s\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\;.

Recall that the random generator
\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}}\mathfrak{q}:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by
(\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}}\mathfrak{q})(\omega,t,x,y):=\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}(\omega,t,y)}\mathfrak{q}(w,t,x,y)=\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{t}(\omega,y)}\mathfrak{q}(t,x,y)
for
y\neq x
, since the generator
\mathfrak{q}
under consideration is deterministic. Denote by
\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}}\mathfrak{q}}
the corresponding jump kernel for
\omega=(\omega_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}\in\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}
and
(t,x)\in[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}
,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}}\mathfrak{q}}(\omega,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle:=\mathds{1}_{\omega_{t-}\neq x}(\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}}\mathfrak{q})(\omega,t,\omega_{t-},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\omega_{t-}\neq x}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}_{t}(\omega,x)}\mathfrak{q}_{t}(\omega_{t-},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}t\;,

then the previous equation rewrites

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[f(\mathbf{X}_{t\wedge\tau})-f(\mathbf{X}_{0})\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau]\times\mathsf{X}}\{f(x)-f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}}\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}}\left[\int_{[0,t\wedge\tau]}(\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\chi}}{\mathfrak{q}})(s)f(\mathbf{X}_{s-})\mathrm{d}s\middle|\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\;.

Choosing
\tau
such that the above terms are meaningful, we conclude that
\mathbb{Q}^{k}_{j}\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathrm{e}^{\chi^{k}_{j}}{\mathfrak{q}})
and finish the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

F.12
.

This proof is an adaptation of Theorem 2.6 in
Léonard (2012)
based on technical lemmas provided above applying on the reference measure
\overrightarrow{R}\in\mathrm{MP}(\overrightarrow{q})
defined at the beginning of Section
F
.
Consider
\|.\|_{\varrho}
defined as

\|\phi\|_{\varrho}:=\inf\left\{a>0\,:\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\phi/a)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]\leqslant 1\right\}\;,

for any
\phi\in S_{\varrho}
, where
S_{\varrho}
is the Orlicz space defined by

\displaystyle S_{\varrho}:=\left\{\phi:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}\,\text{ measurable s.t. }\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(b|\phi|)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]<\infty,\,\text{ for any }b\geqslant 0\right\}\;.

It is well-known that it is a norm called the Luxemburg norm of
S_{\varrho}
.

Furthermore, for any
\chi\in S_{\varrho}
,
by
Theorem

F.8
,
the process

M_{t}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}:=\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi_{s}(\omega,x)\tilde{N}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x)

is a local
\overrightarrow{R}
-martingale. Then,
we define the two stochastic processes
(\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
and
(\mathrm{Z}_{t}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}})_{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]}
as
\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}:=M^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}_{t}-\int_{\left[0,t\right]}\beta_{s}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}\mathrm{d}s
with
\beta_{s}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}:=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi_{s}(\omega,x))\mathrm{n}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}(s,\mathrm{d}x)
, and
\mathrm{Z}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{Y}_{t}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}}
.
By Lemma
F.13
, the process
\mathrm{Z}_{T_{f}}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}
is a
\overrightarrow{R}
-supermartingale, thus
0<\mathbb{E}_{\overrightarrow{R}}\left[\mathrm{Z}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}_{T_{f}}\right]\leqslant 1
.

For
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}})
such that
\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})<\infty
, the Donsker-Varadhan variational formulation of the KL implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})=\sup\left\{\int u\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}-\log\int\mathrm{e}^{u}\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}\,:\,\quad u\text{ measurable and such that }\int\mathrm{e}^{u}\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}<\infty\right\}\;.

(70)

For any
\chi\in S_{\varrho}
, choosing
u=\mathrm{Y}_{T_{f}}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}
and noting that
\log\mathbb{E}_{\overrightarrow{R}}\left[\mathrm{Z}^{\chi,\overrightarrow{q}}_{T_{f}}\right]\leqslant\log 1=0
, we derive

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi_{t}(\omega,x)\tilde{N}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi_{t}(\omega,x))\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}(\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x)\right]\leqslant\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})\;.

Therefore, for any
\chi\in S_{\varrho}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}\right]\leqslant\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})+\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\;.

(71)

For any function
\phi\in S_{\varrho}
, taking
\chi:=\phi/{\|\phi\|_{\varrho}}
in (
71
) implies that for any
\phi\in S_{\varrho}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}\right]\leqslant[\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})+1]\|\phi\|_{\varrho}\;.

(72)

Consider now the sub-space
\mathcal{H}\subset S_{\varrho}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}:=\left\{\phi:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}\,\text{ predictable and bounded s.t. }\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(b|\phi|)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]<\infty,\,\text{ for any }b\geqslant 0\right\}\;.

Since any
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
satisfies (
63
), Lemma
F.13
entails (
72
) for all
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
, as
\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\overrightarrow{R})<\infty
. This implies the linear mapping
\phi\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}\right]
is continuous on
\mathcal{H}
equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\varrho}
.
It is worth noting that
(S_{\varrho},\|\cdot\|_{\varrho})
forms a Banach space
(see
Léonard, 2007
, Proposition 1.18)
, and
\mathcal{H}
is a linear subspace of
S_{\varrho}

(see
Léonard, 2007
, Proposition 1.11)
, hence the Hahn-Banach extension theorem
(
Delatte (2022, Theorem 1)
) ensures that the previously defined linear functional on
\mathcal{H}
can be extended to the entire space
S_{\varrho}
without loss of continuity
since the Luxemburg norm
\|\cdot\|_{\varrho}
is a convex function on
S_{\varrho}
, see
Rao & Ren (1991, Section 2.2)
. Note that the convex conjugate of the Young function
\varrho(|a|)
is
\varrho^{*}(|b|)
. Therefore, as showed in
Rao & Ren (1991, Theorem 3.1.9)
, the dual space of
(S_{\varrho},\|\cdot\|_{\varrho})
is isomorphic to the space

\displaystyle L_{\varrho^{*}}:=\left\{K:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}\text{ measurable s.t. }\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho^{*}(|K|)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]<\infty\right\}\;,

that means there exists some
K\in L_{\varrho^{*}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}K\phi\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right],\quad\text{for any }\phi\in\mathcal{H}\;.

(73)

We now prove the uniqueness and predictability of
K
. Introduce the predictable projection of
K\in L_{\varrho^{*}}
as
K^{pr}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}(K|\mathbf{X}_{[0,t)})
, for
t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right]
. Since
\mathcal{B}
is dense in
S_{\varrho}
,
\mathcal{H}
is dense in the subspace of all the predictable processes in
S_{\varrho}
. Then, any two functions
K_{1}
,
K_{2}\in L_{\varrho^{*}}
satisfying (
73
) must share the same projection,
i.e.
,
K_{1}^{pr}=K_{2}^{pr}
. It follows that there exists a unique predictable process
K
in the space

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(P):=\left\{K:\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}\,\text{ predictable s.t. }\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho^{*}(|K|)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]<\infty\right\}\;,

which satisfies (
73
). Moreover, for any function
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}(\tilde{N}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}}_{\mathbf{X}}-K\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}(N_{\mathbf{X}}-\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}-K\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}(N_{\mathbf{X}}-(K+1)\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}(N_{\mathbf{X}}-U\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}})\;,

with
U=K+1
, and the equation (
73
) is thus equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\phi\mathrm{d}(N_{\mathbf{X}}-U\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{{\overrightarrow{q}}})\right]=0,\quad\text{for any }\phi\in\mathcal{H}\;.

(74)

Thus,
U\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}
is a positive measure and
U
is non-negative. Furthermore, we can argue analogously to obtain equation (
74
) on the interval
[s,t]
for
0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant T_{f}
then choose
\phi_{s}(\omega,x)=f(x)-f(\omega_{s-})
to deduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(\mathbf{X}_{t})-f(\mathbf{X}_{s})\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[s,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}(f(x)-f(\mathbf{X}_{z-}))(U\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}})(\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}x)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{for any }f\in\mathbb{F}(\mathsf{X})}\;.

(75)

Define the random generator
U\overrightarrow{q}
on
\mathbb{D}_{T_{f}}\times[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}^{2}
by
(U\overrightarrow{q})(\omega,t,x,y):=U(\omega,t,y)\overrightarrow{q}(x,y)
for
y\neq x
and use the convention

(U\overrightarrow{q})(\omega,t,x,x):=U(\omega,t,x)\overrightarrow{q}(x,x)=-\sum_{y\neq x}U(\omega,t,y)\overrightarrow{q}(x,y)\;.

Then
U\overrightarrow{q}
forms a generator and (
75
) rewrites

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(\mathbf{X}_{t})-f(\mathbf{X}_{s})\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[s,t\right]}(U\overrightarrow{q})(z)f(\mathbf{X}_{z-})\mathrm{d}z\middle|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right],\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{for any }f\in\mathbb{F}(\mathsf{X})}\;.

As a result, we conclude that
\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{MP}(U\overrightarrow{q})
. We now show the formulation of the Radon-Nikodym density
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}/\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}
. When
\mathbb{P}\sim\overrightarrow{R}
, define the stopping time
\tau_{j}^{k}
as

\displaystyle\tau_{j}^{k}:=\inf\left\{t\in\left[0,T_{f}\right];\int_{\left[0,t\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\log U_{s}(\omega,x)\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s))\geqslant k\text{ or }\log U_{t}(\omega,\omega_{t})\notin[-j,k]\right\}\;,

which coincides with the stopping time
\sigma^{k}_{j}
when
\chi=\log U
. Denote
U^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}:=\mathds{1}_{[0,\sigma^{k}_{j}]}U
and for simplicity, we write
U=U^{\sigma^{k}_{j}}
.
By conditioning w.r.t.
\mathbf{X}_{0}
, we can assume without loss of generality that
\overrightarrow{R}_{0}=\mathbb{P}_{0}
,
i.e.
,
\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{0}}{d\overrightarrow{R}_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0})=1
.

Applying Lemma
F.14
for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{MP}(U\overrightarrow{q})
and
\chi=-\log U
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}:=\mathrm{Z}^{-\log U,U\overrightarrow{q}}_{T_{f}}\mathbb{P}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau_{j}^{k}]}\mathrm{e}^{-\log U}U\overrightarrow{q})=\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau_{j}^{k}]}\overrightarrow{q})\;.

(76)

Furthermore,
\mathbb{Q}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}_{0}=\overrightarrow{R}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}_{0}=\gamma^{d}
, where
\gamma^{d}
is the invariant distribution of
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]}
, which combined with the equation (
76
) imply
\mathbb{Q}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}
is the invariant path measure,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}=\overrightarrow{R}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}\;.

Now, applying first
Lemma

F.14
for
\overrightarrow{R}\in\mathrm{MP}(\overrightarrow{q})
and
\chi=\log U
yields

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}:=\mathrm{Z}^{\log U,\overrightarrow{q}}_{T_{f}}\overrightarrow{R}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau^{k}_{j}]}\mathrm{e}^{\log U}\overrightarrow{q})=\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau^{k}_{j}]}U\overrightarrow{q})\;.

Secondly, applying Lemma
F.14
with
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau^{k}_{j}]}U\overrightarrow{q})
and
\chi=-\log U
implies

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}:=\mathrm{Z}^{-\log U,U\overrightarrow{q}}_{T_{f}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}\in\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau_{j}^{k}]}\mathrm{e}^{-\log U}U\overrightarrow{q})=\mathrm{MP}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau_{j}^{k}]}\overrightarrow{q})\;.

Argue as before, the previous equation together with the initial condition
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}_{0}=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}_{0}=\overrightarrow{R}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}_{0}=\gamma^{d}
yield that
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}=\overrightarrow{R}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}
. Combining it with
\mathbb{Q}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}=\overrightarrow{R}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}
implies

\mathbb{Q}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}=\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}\;,

which means that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Z}^{-\log U,U\overrightarrow{q}}_{T_{f}}\mathbb{P}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}=\mathrm{Z}^{-\log U,U\overrightarrow{q}}_{T_{f}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}\;.

(77)

Now observe that
\mathrm{Z}_{T_{f}}^{-\log U,U\overrightarrow{q}}>0
, therefore, equation (
77
) implies

\mathbb{P}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}
. Hence
\mathbb{P}^{\tau_{j}^{k}}=\mathrm{Z}_{T_{f}}^{\log U,\overrightarrow{q}}\overrightarrow{R}^{\tau^{k}_{j}}
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau_{j}^{k}\wedge T_{f}]}\dfrac{d\mathbb{P}}{d\overrightarrow{R}}((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]})

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau_{j}^{k}\wedge T_{f}]}\dfrac{d\mathbb{P}_{0}}{d\overrightarrow{R}_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0})

\displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{[0,\tau^{k}_{j}\wedge T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}}(\mathds{1}_{[0,\tau^{k}_{j}\wedge T_{f}]}\log U)\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}-\int_{[0,\tau^{k}_{j}\wedge T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\log U)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right)\;.

Letting
k
and
j
tend to infinity, since
\tau:=\lim_{k,j\to\infty}\tau_{j}^{k}=\infty
, we get

\displaystyle\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}}((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]})=\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0})\exp\left(\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\log U\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}-\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\log U)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right)\;.

We now extend the result above to the case when
\mathbb{P}
might not be equivalent to
\overrightarrow{R}
. The idea is to approximate
\mathbb{P}
by a sequence
(\mathbb{P}_{n})
, which satisfies
\mathbb{P}_{n}\sim\overrightarrow{R}
for all
n\geqslant 1
. Denoting

\mathbb{P}_{n}=\left(1-\dfrac{1}{n}\right)\mathbb{P}+\dfrac{\overrightarrow{R}}{n}\quad\text{ for }n\geqslant 1\;,

(78)

we have
\mathbb{P}_{n}\sim\overrightarrow{R}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\mathbb{P}_{n})=0
. For simplicity, we write
\chi=\log U
and
\chi^{n}=\log U^{n}
, which are well-defined
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. From the variational representation given in (
70
) and using
\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{MP}(U\overrightarrow{q})
combined with Lemma
F.13
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\mathbb{P}_{n})

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}(\chi-\chi^{n})\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{U^{n}\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}-\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi-\chi^{n})\mathrm{d}(U^{n}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}})\right]\;.

By definition, we have

\tilde{N}^{U^{n}\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}=N_{\mathbf{X}}-U^{n}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}=N_{\mathbf{X}}-U\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}+(U-U^{n})\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}=\tilde{N}^{U\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}+(U-U^{n})\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\;,

which yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\mathbb{P}_{n})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}(\chi-\chi^{n})\mathrm{d}(\tilde{N}^{U\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}(U-U^{n}))-\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\left(\dfrac{U}{U^{n}}-\log\dfrac{U}{U^{n}}-1\right)U^{n}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}(\chi-\chi^{n})\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{U\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}+\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}U\log\dfrac{U}{U^{n}}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}-\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\left(\dfrac{U}{U^{n}}-1\right)U^{n}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]\;.

Since
\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{MP}(U\overrightarrow{q})
, we deduce that the stochastic integral
\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}(\chi-\chi^{n})\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{U\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}
is a local
\mathbb{P}
-martingale. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\mathbb{P}_{n})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\left(U^{n}-U-U\log\dfrac{U^{n}}{U}\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\left(\dfrac{U^{n}}{U}-\log\dfrac{U^{n}}{U}-1\right)U\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi^{n}-\chi)\mathrm{d}(U\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}})\right]\;.

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}|\mathbb{P}_{n})=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi^{n}-\chi)\mathrm{d}(U\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}})\right]=0\;.

(79)

On the other hand, the fact that
\mathbb{P}_{n}\sim\overrightarrow{R}
yields

\displaystyle\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}}((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]})=\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{n,0}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0})\exp\left(\int_{[0,T_{f}]\times\mathsf{X}}\chi^{n}\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{\overrightarrow{q}}_{\mathbf{X}}-\int_{\left[0,T_{f}\right]\times\mathsf{X}}\varrho(\chi^{n})\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathrm{n}}^{\overrightarrow{q}}\right)\;.

(80)

To obtain the desired expression for the Radon–Nikodym density
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}}
, we represent it as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}}((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{n}}.\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}\overrightarrow{R}}\right)((\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{f}]})
